How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Psychological and neuroscientific research on emotions requires publicly available stimulus materials with normative ratings. However, the necessary rating values for certain dimensions may be lacking when conducting research for specific purposes. In this study, we developed a new affective picture collection, the Animal Affective Picture System (AAPS, pronounced ay-apps), intended for use in research on cuteness or kawaii in Japanese and on human-animal relationships. While cuteness is considered a stimulus attribute with positive valence, kawaii also refers to the feeling people experience when perceiving cuteness. A total of 800 animal images in the public domain or licensed under CC0 (1,000  1,000 pixels) were rated by 914 Japanese adults on a 9-point scale for four dimensions: valence, arousal, motivational direction (approachavoidance), and cuteness/kawaii. Each image was rated by an average of 57 participants. Cuteness ratings were highly correlated with both valence and motivational direction (rs = .97), and the positive correlation remained moderate even when one variable was partialed out (rs = .62 and .64, respectively). These results support the idea that kawaii is a positive emotion associated with approach motivation. The AAPS images, mean rating values, and raw data are freely available for download.

[div-01]

The Animal Affective Picture System (AAPS) 3 Affective images are commonly used in emotion research because they provide a reliable and controlled means of eliciting emotional responses in laboratory settings. Led by the International Affective Picture System (IAPS; Lang et al., 2008), which has been used by many researchers worldwide (Branco et al., 2023), numerous sets of affective images have been developed, such as the Geneva Affective Picture Database (GAPED; Dan-Glauser & Scherer, 2011), the Nencki Affective Picture System (NAPS; Marchewka et al., 2014), and the Open Affective Standardized Image Set (OASIS; Kurdi et al., 2017). Table 1 lists several representative databases.

Table 1

Comparison of Representative Affective Image Databases Note. a The number varies across different versions. b Verbal anchors were provided only in the instructions, not on the scale. These standard affective image sets have several limitations. First, although these databases commonly contain valence and arousal ratings, they often lack the scales necessary for specialized research themes. For example, the IAPS contains the dominance scale, the NAPS contains the motivational direction scale, and the GAPED contains the moral/ethical acceptability and legal acceptability scales, but ratings on other dimensions were unavailable. Although a new dimension can be added in a subsequent study, such as multiple dimensional ratings for IAPS images (Libkuman et al., 2007) and beauty ratings for OASIS images (Brielmann & Pelli, 2019), doing so is not easy because it essentially means repeating the survey at the same scale.

Second, although there are many images in total, the number of images suitable for a specific purpose may be smaller than needed. For example, NAPS consists of 221 animals, 372 faces, 185 landscapes, 328 objects, and 250 people. OASIS consists of 134 animals, 200 objects, 346 people, and 220 scenes. Because the content, composition, and number of subjects depicted in each category vary considerably, attempting to control them may result in retaining only a small number of images. This could be problematic for research requiring a large number of stimulus images, such as memory research and event-related potential studies. Therefore, it is ultimately necessary to create datasets tailored to specific research purposes, such as clinical phobias (Dan-Glauser & Scherer, 2011; Michałowski et al., 2017) and combat-related psychopathology (Goodman et al., 2016), and to make them publicly available to allow other researchers in these specific areas to use them.

In this study, we created a new database consisting of 800 animal images that can be used for research on the perception of cuteness and its psychological effects (e.g., Borgi & Cirulli, 2016; Nittono et al., 2012; Sherman et al., 2009) and on human-animal relationships in general (e.g., Ein et al., 2019; Liu et al., 2025; Thomas-Walters et al., 2020; Turner-Collins & Breitenbecher, 2019).

Cuteness and Kawaii

Interest in cuteness, or kawaii in Japanese, has grown in various fields, including humanities (Dale, 2023), psychology (Sherman & Haidt, 2011), neuroscience (Kringelbach et al., 2016), consumer marketing (Shin & Mattila, 2021), moral and environmental education (Lu et al., in press; Zhang & Zhou, 2020), social media (Golonka et al., 2023), engineering (Marcus et al., 2017; Ohkura, 2019), and robotics (Shiomi, 2025). Most studies have cited Lorenz's (1943) concept of the Kindchenschema (baby schema), positing that specific stimulus features elicit positive emotional responses. It has been shown that not only human children but also animal faces and industrial products (e.g., car fronts) are perceived as cute when they share the same morphological features as humans (Borgi et al., 2014; Little, 2012; Miesler et al., 2011).

Recent studies have indicated that visual features perceived as cute extend beyond a single entity. People tend to rate two figures as cuter than a single figure (Shiomi et al., 2023), and they also rate figures as cuter when they are being touched by others (Ohashi & Nittono, 2026) or when they are in affectionate social relationships (Steinnes et al., 2019).

Another type of cuteness, called whimsical cuteness, has been proposed and is characterized by fun and playfulness rather than care and protection (Nenkov & Scott, 2014; Scott & Nenkov, 2016). The Kindchenschema cuteness and whimsical cuteness appear to have different effects on behavior (Lei et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2026). The concept of the Kindchenschema has been critically examined (Kawaguchi & Waller, 2024) and extended in terms of developmental investment (Garsmeur & Baumard, in press).

The Japanese word "kawaii" refers to a broader range of subjects than the English word "cute" (Nittono, 2016; Nittono et al., 2023) because it describes not only the physical features of an object but also the emotion felt toward it (Lieber-Milo, 2025). English lacks a word that directly describes the emotion felt when perceiving cuteness (Buckley, 2016). This emotion has been described as "tenderness" (Kalawski, 2010) and "nurturant love" (O'Neil et al., 2018), and with the Sanskrit term "kama muta" (Steinnes et al., 2019 (Steinnes et al., , 2024)).

The terms "kawaii" and "cute" are used almost synonymously when describing infants or animals. It has been shown that the corresponding psychological states, measured by the Self-Assessment Manikin (SAM) nonverbal graphic scale (Bradley & Lang, 1994), are common to Japan, the United States, and Argentina (Nittono et al., 2023). Hereafter, we use the term "cuteness" to refer to both the physical features of an object and the feeling associated with perceiving them.

Previous studies on cuteness have often involved researchers creating stimuli based on the baby schema hypothesis, which holds that there is a physical dimension along which cuteness perception varies systematically (Kawaguchi & Waller, 2024; Lorenz, 1943). In the case of infant faces, for example, some studies have artificially increased or decreased the characteristics of the baby schema (Borgi et al., 2014; Glocker et al., 2009; Löwenbrück & Hess, 2021). Others have created images by modifying faces along a continuum between high-and low-cuteness average faces, the latter of which are created based on subjective ratings (Hahn et al., 2013; Lobmaier et al., 2010; Sprengelmeyer et al., 2009).

For both methods, a full set of images is rarely freely accessible because of portrait rights issues. This problem can be circumvented by using composite faces. Nittono et al.

(2022) employed the second method mentioned above and published the Japanese Cute Infant Face (JCIF) dataset. The dataset contains high-and low-cuteness prototypical faces of six-month-old infants in Japan, as well as 50 composite faces with normative cuteness ratings and their cuteness-augmented and cuteness-decreased versions.

In this study, we created a new image set for cuteness research by collecting The Animal Affective Picture System (AAPS) 8 nonhuman animal images. Animal images are popular stimulus materials in this field (Álvarez-San Millán et al., 2022; Fox & Ziv, 2022; Nakamura et al., 2025; Nittono et al., 2012; Sherman et al., 2009; Stavropoulos & Alba, 2018; Takamatsu & Nittono, in press; Yoshikawa et al., 2020; Yoshikawa & Masaki, 2021; Ziv & Fox, 2022). The baby schema present in animals can also be perceived as cute (Golle et al., 2013; Little, 2012), and young animal faces receive higher cuteness ratings than human infant faces (Borgi et al., 2014).

Younger people, in particular, are more likely to evaluate animal babies as cuter than human infants (Nittono et al., 2021).

The use of animal image stimuli has additional advantages. First, the variety of images can be larger for animal images than for human facial images. Cortical activity in response to face images is attenuated by repetition because of category-specific suppression (Mercure et al., 2011; Schweinberger & Neumann, 2016). Although it is unclear whether the cuteness ratings of infant faces are affected by the inclusion of other types of stimuli, stimulus context and adaptation certainly affect cuteness perception.

For example, human infant faces are rated as less cute or cuter depending on whether participants viewed cuter or less cute human and animal faces beforehand (Golle et al., 2013).

Second, the perception and processing of human infant faces may differ according to the models' ethnicity (Hodsoll et al., 2010; Spencer et al., 2018, but see Proverbio et al., 2011; Proverbio & De Gabriele, 2019 for null results) and sex (Hildebrandt & Fitzgerald, 1979). In contrast, aesthetic evaluation and preference for animal images are less variable across cultures (Frynta et al., 2011 (Frynta et al., , 2014)), although attitudes toward animals and animal welfare may vary across geographically and culturally diverse countries (Sinclair et al., 2022). Therefore, in addition to infant facial images, a dataset of animal images with normative ratings would be beneficial for cuteness research.

Similar attempts have been made to create animal image datasets. The Animal Images Database (Animal.ID; Possidónio et al., 2019) contains 120 photographs of single animals on a white background, with rating values on a 7-point scale for 11 dimensions, including cuteness (see Table 1). Nittono et al. (2025) exploratorily created a set of 75 photographic images of animals and obtained normative ratings of valence, arousal, motivational direction (approach-avoidance), and cuteness on a 9-point scale.

However, because both sets contain a relatively small number of images, they are insufficient for studies that require a greater variety of stimuli.

Present Study

To expand on previous work, the aim was to create a new database of 800 photographs of animals. This number was chosen because future research in preparation required 200 images for each of the high-and low-cuteness conditions, which correspond to the top and bottom quarters. With a particular focus on research into cuteness, many images of companion animals (especially dogs and cats) were included, and clearly unpleasant images were excluded so that high-cuteness and low-cuteness (i.e., neutral) images could be selected. Only images depicting a single animal were included to control for social factors that emerge when multiple individuals coexist in an image and affect cuteness ratings (Shiomi et al., 2023; Steinnes et al., 2019). To avoid copyright issues and ensure that the images could be freely used, we collected only photographs licensed under Creative Commons Zero (CC0) or in the public domain. We adopted a square image format, rather than a landscape or portrait format, because stimulus images are often viewed on smartphones nowadays. To ensure a certain level of consistency in image quality, we kept the file size constant.

Another research question in this study concerns the relationship between the perception of cuteness and other affective dimensions. Previous research has shown that cuteness ratings are strongly correlated with subjective pleasure and approach motivation (Nittono & Ihara, 2017). The association between cuteness and approach motivation is reflected in postural responses, such as leaning forward when viewing cute animal pictures on a force plate (Nakamura et al., 2025). Similarly, people gaze at cute stimuli for longer periods than at less cute stimuli (Hahn et al., 2013; Nittono & Ihara, 2017; Parsons et al., 2011). Based on these findings, Nittono (2016) defined the kawaii emotion as a positive affect associated with approach motivation. However, it remains unclear whether valence and motivational direction independently account for cuteness. There is a linear positive correlation between ratings of valence and motivational direction for affective pictures (e.g., r = .98 for animal pictures in NAPS, calculated from the supplementary material; Marchewka et al., 2014), but the two are not the same (Gable & Dreisbach, 2021). There are forms of pleasure that do not involve high approach motivation (e.g., contentment; Cordaro et al., 2016), and there are forms of approach motivation that are not pleasurable (e.g., anger; Carver & Harmon-Jones, 2009). Therefore, cuteness is expected to correlate with both of these factors but cannot be explained by either factor alone.

With regard to another affective dimension, arousal, the findings are conflicting.

Images of babies and young animals have been consistently rated as eliciting moderate to low arousal in existing image databases, such as IAPS and NAPS. This tendency has been observed regardless of perceivers' gender in Japan, the United States, and Argentina (Nittono et al., 2023). In contrast, cute stimuli can evoke strong positive emotions and trigger aggression-like behavior called cute aggression (Aragón et al., 2015; Stavropoulos & Alba, 2018). It has also been shown that viewing cute stimuli elicits not only smiles but also other facial expressions, such as furrowed brows (O'Neil et al., 2026), suggesting that cuteness-related emotions are complex. Data from Animal.ID revealed a strong linear positive correlation between arousal and cuteness ratings for 120 photographs (r =. 84, calculated from the supplementary material of Possidónio et al., 2019). This study aimed to examine the relationship between cuteness and arousal using a different set of animal images.

Method

Stimulus Curation

First, more than 1,400 animal images were collected from three websites: Pixnio (https://pixnio.com), the Smithsonian Institution (https://www.si.edu/spotlight/open-access-animals), and the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS; https://www.fws.gov/search/images). All images were verified to be in the public domain or distributed under CC0. AI-generated images were excluded when searching Pixnio.

Second, images were excluded if they met any of the following criteria: (1) they showed multiple individuals; (2) they included well-known locations or trademarks; (3) they contained legible text; (4) they had low resolution; (5) it was difficult to identify the animal's head; or (6) they were extremely bright or dark. The same subject appeared no more than three times, each time with a different composition. The final 800 images selected were manually cropped to a square and then adjusted to a resolution of 1,000  1,000 and a file size of 100 KB (95-104 KB). The mean brightness (luma), converted from RGB values using the equation 0.299*R + 0.587*G + 0.114*B [ITU-R BT.601], was 109.7 (SD = 28.7, range = 32.1-188.1) on the grayscale (0-255). Lakens et al. (2013)used the simple average of RGB values as an index of image brightness, but a weighted average was used here to adjust for human spectral sensitivity. In the present study, the correlation between the two was r = .99.

The edited JPG file, original URL, and mean image brightness of each image are available at OSF (https://osf.io/3uzrw). The image files were named according to the following rule: The first digit indicates arbitrary animal categories (1: dogs, 2: cats, 3: small-sized mammals, 4: medium-and large-sized mammals, 5: primates, 6: aquatic mammals, 7: birds, 8: reptiles and amphibians, 9: fish and others), and the next three digits indicate the serial numbers of images for each website (000-199 for Pixnio, 200-399 for Smithsonian, and 400-599 for FWS). For example, 1000.jpg is a dog image from Pixnio.

Participants

The survey was conducted using Qualtrics between March 20 and 23, 2026. A total of 1,000 participants were recruited from Lancers, Inc. (Tokyo, Japan), with the following requirements: (1) They had normal or corrected-to-normal vision, (2) they were native Japanese speakers, and (3) they used a Windows PC. Language restrictions were set to ensure that the respondents adequately understood the question items. Device restrictions were imposed to ensure that the screen layout and formatting remained consistent. Each participant was allowed to participate only once and received an honorarium of 360 yen for their participation. The research protocol was approved by the University of Osaka Graduate School of Human Sciences Research Ethics Committee (HB025-166, March 12, 2026). All participants provided electronic informed consent.

Rating Scales

The following four 9-point scales were presented on a single page: (1) Valence. "I'm judging this image as ..." (1 = very negative,5 = neutral,9 = very positive). (2) Arousal. "Confronted with this image, I'm feeling: ..." (1 = calm,5 = neutral,9 = excited). (3) Motivational direction. "My reaction to this image is ..." (1 = to avoid,5 = neutral,9 = to approach). (4) Cuteness/kawaii. "This image is ..." (1 = not at all kawaii,9 = extremely kawaii). The scale descriptions of valence and arousal were adopted from the GAPED and NAPS, except for the change in subject from "you are" to "I'm" to conform to Japanese grammar. The arousal scale labels followed those of Arousal 1 of the GAPED. The scale description and labels of motivational direction were adopted from the NAPS. The scale description and labels of cuteness were newly created. Note that this scale is unipolar without a neutral point. In the former dataset (Nittono et al., 2025), the motivational direction scale ranged from -4 to +4, and the cuteness scale ranged from 0 to 8. However, we aligned our scoring method with those of other databases, making all ranges from 1 to 9.

Procedure

Participants completed the questionnaire using their own computers. Each trial consisted of a picture displayed for 3 s, and the subsequent rating screen containing the four scales appeared with a thumbnail of the image at the top. At the beginning of the survey, participants were asked to adjust the zoom level of their browser window so that the rating screen would fit on a single screen, allowing them to answer without having to scroll. Each participant rated 50 images randomly selected from the 800 images. The main rating session was preceded by two practice trials and followed by two quality-check trials. For the practice and quality-check trials, one positive and one negative image were each selected from the previous dataset (Nittono et al., 2025). Because the two qualitycheck trials seamlessly followed the main rating session and the participants did not know how many trials were left, they could not distinguish them from the main rating trials.

Exclusion Criteria

Participants who did not rate the positive image as higher than the negative image in the quality-check trials for valence, motivational direction, or cuteness were removed because their ratings were considered inaccurate or atypical. Arousal ratings were not used as exclusion criteria because there was no expected direction.

Statistical Analysis

For each of the 800 images, the mean, standard deviation, and 95% confidence interval of each rating value were calculated to obtain the normative values. Scatter plots and Pearson correlation coefficients (r) were calculated using the images as units between the four scales. Quadratic regression curves were fitted to the data. The regression coefficients were calculated using mean-centered values. To examine whether these correlations existed at the intra-individual level, within-participant repeated-measures correlation coefficients (r rm ) were also calculated between scales using the rmcorr R package (Bakdash & Marusich, 2017). We did not analyze men's and women's responses separately because there was no reason to expect a particular gender/sex to give higher/lower ratings to a particular set of animal images.

Because the cuteness score was expected to correlate with both valence and motivational direction, partial correlations were calculated after controlling for each variable's effects. Due to multicollinearity (i.e., an extremely high correlation between valence and motivational direction), we did not conduct a multiple regression analysis to explain the cuteness scores using the other scales.

To examine the relationship with physical properties, correlations between the mean brightness and respective scales were calculated on an exploratory basis. The dataset and R script used for the calculations are available at OSF (https://osf.io/3uzrw).

Results

After excluding 86 participants according to the exclusion criteria, 914 participants (558 men, 355 women, 1 other; M = 46.0 years old, SD =10.6, range =18-79) were included in the analysis. On average, each image was rated by 57 participants (range = 35-85). The median time taken to complete the survey was 18.2 min.

Figure 1 shows histograms for the four rating scales and scatter plots of the relationships between valence, arousal, motivational direction, and cuteness ratings of the 800 images. Table 2 presents the simple and within-participant correlation coefficients, the B coefficients of the fitted quadratic regression equations, and the results of the significance tests. Strong positive correlations were observed between valence, motivation, and cuteness scores, both at the image level (r > .94) and at the intra-individual level (r rm > .77). Repeated-measures correlations with cuteness were higher for motivational direction, r rm = .86, 95% CI [.857, .862] than for valence, r rm = .81, 95% CI [.805, .811]. The Animal Affective Picture System (AAPS) 16 Note. Zero-order Pearson's correlation coefficients and best-fitting quadratic regression lines are shown.

Table 2 Although a quadratic relationship was observed between arousal and each scale, the overall determination coefficient was low (adjusted R² < .21). A quadratic relationship was also found between cuteness and valence or arousal, but the increase in the proportion of variance explained by the quadratic term was small (ΔR² < .03).

The correlation between cuteness and valence (r = .97) remained significant even after partialing out the effect of motivational direction (r = .64, p < .001). Similarly, the correlation between cuteness and motivational direction (r = .97) remained significant even after controlling for the effect of valence (r = .62, p < .001). Figure 2 shows scatter plots of the residuals for the relationship between cuteness and valence and between cuteness and motivational direction. Figure 3 shows examples of images with mean normative ratings of 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, and 8 on the 9-point cuteness scale (1 to 9). In general, images of small mammals received high scores, whereas those of amphibians and reptiles received low scores. This figure suggests that images with high cuteness scores are brighter than those with low cuteness scores. Figure 4 shows scatter plots of the relationship between image brightness and rating scale values. Weak positive correlations were found for valence, motivational direction, and cuteness ratings. Note. Images with mean ratings closest to each score are shown.

Discussion

This study produced normative rating values for 800 animal images available on the internet. The ranges for cuteness, pleasantness, and motivational direction were broad. As expected, the mean ratings were slightly above neutral, with more positive images than negative ones. Conversely, arousal was confined to a narrow range around the center of the rating scale. Therefore, this stimulus set is suitable for studying cuteness while controlling for the arousal of stimuli.

The cuteness score showed a similarly high simple correlation with both valence and approach motivation (rs = .97 and .97). Valence and approach motivation were also highly correlated (r = .95), consistent with NAPS (r = .98; Marchewka et al., 2014). In the quadratic regression analysis, a significant quadratic relationship was found with valence, whereas only a linear relationship was observed with approach motivation. As shown in Figure 1 (upper right panel), the quadratic relationship between cuteness and valence scores stems from the fact that valence scores are higher when cuteness scores are moderate (i.e., the curve is convex upward). Motivational direction appeared to be more linearly related to cuteness than valence was. In the repeated-measures correlation analysis (correlation within individuals), motivational direction was more strongly correlated with cuteness than with valence. Since pleasant stimuli include categories other than cuteness, there are likely images with positive valence and moderate cuteness, such as those evoking emotions of contentment or awe (Cordaro et al., 2016; Shiota & Keltner, 2007). However, the proportion explained by the quadratic term was small and negligible in the present study.

The correlation between cuteness and valence remained significant even after controlling for the influence of motivational direction through partial correlation analysis. Similarly, the correlation between cuteness and approach motivation remained significant, even after controlling for the influence of valence. Although a linear relationship was observed between cuteness and motivation, the partial correlation analysis results indicated that approach motivation alone could not explain cuteness scores; some cute stimuli elicit a relatively low approach motivation. For example, although colorful reptiles and the babies of wild animals such as bears and lions are perceived as cute, physically approaching them is dangerous.

Previous studies have suggested that fear and beauty are perceived independently: Fear and beauty ratings are positively correlated among snakes (Landová et al., 2018) but negatively correlated among other reptiles with legs (Janovcová et al., 2019). Furthermore, stimuli that elicit high approach motivation may be perceived as less cute when they have a negative valence. For example, anger is an approach-motivated negative emotion (Carver & Harmon-Jones, 2009). These results indicate that positive valence and approach motivation, considered separately, are insufficient to explain cuteness. This is consistent with Nittono's (2016) definition of kawaii as a positive emotion associated with approach motivation.

One potential problem is that the term "approach-avoidance" has multiple meanings (Elliot, 2006). It can refer to a physical or behavioral approach, which involves action or preparation for action, or a psychological approach, such as the desire to get involved. The latter may be closer to the concept of interest (Silvia, 2006). Interest arises in response to both pleasant and unpleasant stimuli, and traditionally, viewing duration has been regarded as a behavioral indicator of interest (Berlyne, 1949). Lang et al. (1993) demonstrated that the amount of time people spent viewing IAPS pictures correlated with the pictures' arousal ratings. Previous studies have shown that greater cuteness is associated with a longer viewing duration in both key-press tasks, in which participants press a button to lengthen or shorten their viewing time (Hahn et al., 2013; Parsons et al., 2011), and free-viewing tasks, in which participants determine when they stop viewing (Nittono & Ihara, 2017).

In some cases, subjective and behavioral approach responses diverge. Cute animal images elicit both a forward body posture and a subjective desire to approach (Nakamura et al., 2025). However, infant faces associated with positive personality traits, which were demonstrated to be rated as cuter (Takamatsu et al., 2023), increased the subjective wanting to approach but did not induce a leaning forward postural response (Nakamura & Nittono, under review). In future studies, a more sophisticated definition of "approach" in the response scale is required, including a distinction between the subjective and physical aspects of approach motivation.

The arousal ratings in this study were generally moderate, with no extreme values.

Arousal was not linearly related to cuteness, pleasure, or motivational direction. Quadratic relationships were found to be significant, although the explained variance was low. The weak quadratic (V-shaped) relationship between valence and arousal, where arousal is lowest for neutral stimuli with moderate valence and increases for both pleasant and unpleasant stimuli, is a commonly observed pattern in subjective experiences (Kuppens et al., 2013). There are also cultural differences in the standard V-shaped angle, with its steepness weaker in Eastern countries, such as Hong Kong, Korea, and Japan, than in Western countries, such as Canada and Spain (Kuppens et al., 2017). This is consistent with the current observation in Japan that there is almost no relationship between valence and arousal.

The interpretation of the arousal scale varies across studies (Marchewka et al., 2014). In the original IAPS study, the arousal scale was administered as a bipolar scale (with a neutral response at the midpoint); however, in practice, the negative extreme (calm) was sometimes interpreted as no response (Ribeiro et al., 2005). Similarly, in the GAPED, the scores from two scales (Calm to Excited, Stimulated to Relaxed) are averaged and recalculated into a unipolar arousal scale (ranging from "insignificantly" to "highly arousing"). In this study, since the mode of the scale scores was 4 or 5, the midpoint appeared to be correctly interpreted as neutral or no change, as intended.

The present study found no correlation between cuteness and arousal (r = .00), which contradicts previous research. In the Animal.ID (Possidónio et al., 2019), based on Portuguese participants, there were strong positive linear correlations between arousal and cuteness ratings (r = .84) as well as between arousal and valence (r = .67) for 120 photographs, calculated from the item-based mean scores in the supplementary material.

Previous studies have shown that the correlation between valence and arousal is either nonexistent (r = -.03 in this study; r = -.06 in the OASIS, reported in the text) or negative (r = -.84 in the NAPS; r = -.85 in the GAPED, both calculated from the supplementary materials). A negative correlation is observed when low arousal is interpreted as positive valence. In contrast, Possidónio et al.'s (2019) data showed disproportionately high arousal ratings in response to positive stimuli. These differences may be due to differences in the visual materials and rating methods used. The strength of correlation may depend on the variety of images used in a single rating session. The Animal.ID images appear uniform because the original background was replaced with a white one, and the full body of a single animal was positioned to face the right. Because these images are simple and easy to process (Bradley et al., 2007), their semantic content may influence ratings more than their visual features. Furthermore, because each participant rated images across 11 subjective judgment dimensions, confusion may have arisen between the different dimensions. The results of this study did not provide evidence that greater cuteness leads to greater arousal, at least when still images are used. More realistic stimuli, such as videos or real-world objects, may induce a more intense, arousing state, as suggested by the phenomenon of cute aggression (Aragón et al., 2015). It is worth noting that cute images did not have calming effects, either. Cute images of babies and young animals were associated with moderate arousal. This tendency is consistent with the existing image databases, such as IAPS and NAPS, and has been observed regardless of perceivers' gender or culture (Nittono et al., 2023).

The finding that bright photographs are positively evaluated is consistent with previous research (Lakens et al., 2013; Specker et al., 2018; Specker & Leder, 2018). In this study, the collected images were cropped and resized; however, their color tones were not altered.

Because brightness may influence neural responses such as ERP (especially early components; Johannes et al., 1995) and pupil diameter (Mathôt, 2018), it would be preferable to control for it. As the correlations are not strong (r = .30-.34), pleasant or cute stimuli can be selected after adjusting brightness within a certain range. Because these values differ from the actual luminance on the display, strict measurements are required when necessary.

Limitations and Future Tasks

This study has several limitations. First, the survey was conducted exclusively in a Japanese cohort. Although previous research has demonstrated that the concepts of cute and kawaii concerning infants and young animals are nearly identical in Japan, the United States, and Argentina (Nittono et al., 2021 (Nittono et al., , 2023)), it may be safer to confirm how the images are evaluated in other populations. Moreover, attitudes toward animals may vary across cultures (Sinclair et al., 2022), and caution is warranted regarding the generalizability of the present findings.

Second, this study did not distinguish between different types of cuteness. Although it primarily addresses prototypical cuteness based on the baby schema, it is possible that evaluations of cuteness were made on different dimensions depending on the photograph.

There are other types of cuteness, such as whimsical cuteness (Nenkov & Scott, 2014; Scott & Nenkov, 2016) and types without baby schema features (Nittono & Ihara, 2017). Since whimsical cuteness is not defined by distinctive physical characteristics, whether these stimuli are considered cute is likely to be more strongly influenced by culture and age groups. In future research, it may be useful to assess perceived youthfulness or infantility along with cuteness ratings to differentiate baby schema cuteness from other types of cuteness.

Third, because this study assessed only basic affective dimensions, it is unclear how cuteness relates to other psychological dimensions. Possidónio et al. (2019) showed that people perceive cuter animals as less dangerous, more capable of feeling and thinking, and more likely to elicit feelings of care. For a theoretical understanding of cuteness, it is important to consider its relationship with other dimensions, such as mentalizing, care, and cognitive appraisals of learning potential or sociability (Garsmeur & Baumard, in press; Sherman & Haidt, 2011).

Conclusion

This study provides a set of 800 public domain/CC0 photographs of individual animals with normative 9-point ratings of valence, arousal, motivational direction, and cuteness. Although the ratings were made by the Japanese adult population, which may restrict their generalizability, the AAPS can be used for future research on cuteness and human-animal relationships, including companion animals, wildlife, and zoo education. This study also demonstrates that kawaii, or the feeling of perceiving cuteness, is a positive emotion associated with approach motivation. Additionally, it shows that cute images induce a moderate state of arousal, which is neither calm nor excited.
